beyond the Anglophone markets — Indian, Singaporean, Israeli, Ukrainian, Saudi, Chinese and
Japanese reviewers all write in it. Country-based segmentation and language-based segmentation
are different operations and give different answers.

## 6.11 Storefronts below 50 reviews — limited evidence

64 storefronts have fewer than 50 reviews and receive **no standalone
claims**. Their reviews are fully included in every global number.

Two observations are recorded with explicit limited-evidence warnings because they are
coherent and corroborated elsewhere:

- **Taiwan (16 reviews) — Traditional Chinese is missing.** #1173 (`2565901316`),
  #1210 (`2880986730`), #1114 (`2283239198`), #1121 (`2293228457`), #1532 (`5692422621`).
  ⚠️ *Limited evidence: 16 reviews. But it is corroborated by the CN localisation
  complaints (§6.4) and by the listing, which offers Simplified Chinese only (§E1).*
- **Korea (7 reviews) — no Korean-language complaint, but three of seven reviews ask
  for a widget or a frequency option.** ⚠️ *Limited evidence: 7 reviews. Recorded
  only because it matches the global pattern exactly.*

Everything else below the threshold is left uninterpreted.

---

# PART 7 — TIME-TREND ANALYSIS

**This is the most important part of this report.** 45.32% of the corpus is from era
E1; every global percentage in Parts 3–6 is therefore weighted towards how the app behaved in
2016–17. What follows is what actually happened.

## 7.1 Method

Six eras, with boundaries set from the **product timeline** — shipped releases and datable
product events — not from the shape of the data. Release dates from March 2020 onward are from
Apple's public version history (§E4); earlier ones are inferred from reviews and labelled.

| Era | Window | Reviews | Reviews/month | Mean | What the product did |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---|
| **E1** Launch and first year | 2016-03-09 → 2017-04-30 | 809 | 59.1 | **4.16** | 1.0 ships 8 Mar 2016; free tier 1→3 habits (Mar); multi-check-in ships (Sep); iOS-10 widget ships (Oct) |
| **E2** Watch era | 2017-05-01 → 2018-10-31 | 473 | 26.3 | **4.14** | Apple Watch app ships (~May 2017); free tier reaches 5 habits (by Nov 2017) |
| **E3** Redesign and decay | 2018-11-01 → 2020-08-24 | 308 | 14.2 | **3.59** | Nov-2018 check-in redesign; severe performance regression; first abandonment claims |
| **E4** Silence and the lost widget | 2020-08-25 → 2022-11-09 | 123 | 4.6 | **3.51** | 374-day release gap; iOS-14 home-screen widget never ships; NC widget removed Sep 2021, restored Nov 2021; dark mode ships Oct 2021; subscription pricing arrives |
| **E5** Abandonment | 2022-11-10 → 2025-09-03 | 59 | 1.7 | **3.07** | **1,030 days with no release at all**, beginning with a build that would not launch |
| **E6** Revival | 2025-09-04 → 2026-07-13 | 13 | 1.3 | **3.38** | 1.10 ships 4 Sep 2025 (iOS 18 minimum); 11 releases in 11 months |

Review volume per month collapses by a factor of **45×** from E1 to E6. The
mean rating falls monotonically across five consecutive eras, then ticks up slightly in E6 on
13 reviews.

⚠️ **E5 (59 reviews) and E6 (13 reviews) are thin.** One review moves E6
by 7.7 points. Every E5/E6 percentage below is directional only.

## 7.2 Theme movement across eras

Percentages are **of that era's reviews**.

| Theme | E1 | E2 | E3 | E4 | E5 | E6 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| `U:praise_any` | 77.9% | 66.2% | 49.0% | 43.9% | 33.9% | 38.5% |
| `PR_DESIGN` | 32.5% | 23.3% | 18.8% | 14.6% | 15.2% | 15.4% |
| `PR_SIMPLE` | 25.1% | 22.4% | 10.1% | 11.4% | 8.5% | 15.4% |
| `PR_OUTCOME` | 6.9% | 5.5% | 4.9% | 4.9% | 1.7% | 15.4% |
| `U:bug_any` | 12.4% | 29.4% | 46.4% | 43.1% | 50.9% | 30.8% |
| `U:reliability` | 7.3% | 14.2% | 20.8% | 15.4% | 35.6% | 30.8% |
| `BUG_PERF` | 0.7% | 3.4% | 8.8% | 8.9% | 16.9% | 0.0% |
| `U:dataloss_any` | 0.7% | 4.2% | 6.5% | 4.9% | 5.1% | 0.0% |
| `DEV_STALE` | 0.6% | 1.1% | 10.7% | 8.9% | 20.3% | 7.7% |
| `U:paywall_complaint` | 16.2% | 5.1% | 6.5% | 12.2% | 6.8% | 15.4% |
| `U:churn_any` | 2.7% | 5.7% | 8.4% | 11.4% | 13.6% | 7.7% |
| `REQ_WIDGET` | 2.0% | 0.6% | 1.0% | 10.6% | 16.9% | 15.4% |
| `REQ_DASHBOARD` | 1.7% | 4.4% | 7.5% | 7.3% | 1.7% | 15.4% |
| *(denominator: reviews in that era)* | *809* | *473* | *308* | *123* | *59* | *13* |

The three lines that matter:

- **Praise falls from 77.87% to 33.9%.**
- **Defect reports rise from 12.36% to 50.85%** — by E5
  a *majority* of reviews report something broken.
- **Abandonment claims go from 0.62% to 20.34%.**

Note that `PR_DESIGN` never collapses — it holds at 15.25% even in E5. The
app never stopped being beautiful. It stopped working.

## 7.3 The five crash waves

Each is datable and each is visible as a cluster in the corpus.

### Wave 1 — launch, March 2016

Today shipped 8 March 2016 as an **Apple Editors' Choice** (`MKT_EDITORS` 6
reviews mention the feature) and crashed immediately for a large fraction of users.
21 reviews dated 2016-03-09 → 2016-03-31 report a crash or launch failure.

> "When I saw this was an editors choice app I checked it out… all I get is the screen that
> says today. And then everything crashes." — #8, `1345892603`, us, 1★
> "**Great crash simulator**… All it does is crash" — #71, `1346499392`, us, 1★

Combined with the one-habit paywall, this produced the worst month in the app's history:
**209 reviews in 2016-03**, mean 3.11★, and
54 reviews on 2016-03-11 alone. The app recovered — by mid-2017 the
monthly mean was above 4.5 — but the launch left 228 reviews (12.77% of
the entire corpus) at mean 3.25★ permanently attached to the listing.

### Wave 2 — iOS 9/10, October–November 2016

18 launch-crash reviews between 2016-10-20 and 2016-12-01, on both iOS 9 and iOS 10.

> "New update left the app useless. Now it won't open so you can't use it on iOS 10" — #503, `1470204089`, gb, 1★
> "花了钱内购以后就再也没好好打开过程序，一打开5秒内闪退，**建议ios9的都谨慎考虑内购**" (*after paying for
> the IAP the app has never opened properly; crashes within 5 seconds. I'd advise iOS 9 users to
> think carefully before buying*) — #587, `1486760895`, cn, 1★

### Wave 3 — Face ID / Touch ID, March 2018

A tight cluster: 5 reviews in nine days, mostly Chinese, all reporting that
enabling the biometric lock — **a paid feature** — made the app crash on entry.

> "因为疏忽付了完整版的30元…开了指纹解锁后按指纹闪退，输密码也闪退，**我这30元是干嘛的**，付了三十元就让我删掉应用吗？给苹果发申诉，
> 说不能退回" (*I carelessly paid 30 yuan for the full version… after enabling fingerprint unlock,
> pressing the fingerprint crashes, entering the password also crashes. What was my 30 yuan for?
> I appealed to Apple and was told no refund*) — #1103, `2273762627`, cn, 1★

The same fortnight also carried the purchase outage (§5.4). March 2018 is the month this app
broke its paying users on two axes at once.

### Wave 4 — iOS 12 / watchOS 5, September–October 2018

7 reviews between 2018-09-20 and 2018-11-01 report the Watch app broken by the OS
update.

> "Broken since iOS 12 / watchOS 5… it can't run for more than a few seconds without crashing
> since the latest OS came out and is essentially useless at this stage. **App hasn't had an
> update in nearly 2 months, what's going on guys?**" — #1258, `3226036476`, gb, 1★

### Wave 5 — the black screen, November 2022

The most consequential, because of what followed. Version **1.9.63 shipped 2022-11-09** (§E4).
The first black-screen review is dated **2022-11-09**. 14 reviews between 2022-11-09
and 2023-12-31 report the app opening to a blank or black screen, or not launching.

> "Running iOS 16.1, unable to use the app whatsoever. The screen is just blank then crashes.
> Been using the app for about 2 years and had been wonderful till the latest iOS update."
> — #1713, `9272101069`, us, 3★
> "アプリ開けたら黒い画面のみで全く使えなくなりました。とても気に入っているアプリだし、**長年チェックイン
> した記録もある**ので是非改善してもらいたい" (*when I open the app there's only a black screen and it's
> completely unusable. I love this app and have years of check-in records, so I really want this
> fixed*) — #1716, `9295872427`, jp, 1★

**Apple's version history shows the next release was 1.10 on 2025-09-04 — 1,030 days
later.** The app shipped a build that would not launch for a substantial number of users and
then did not ship again for two years and ten months.

**IDs across all five waves:** `1345878357`, `1345892096`, `1345892603`, `1345960203`, `1346015418`, `1346100690`, `1346374146`, `1346492356`, `1346532867`, `1346604080`, `1346659051`, `1470204089`, `1476921895`, `1478223591`.

## 7.4 The 2018 redesign — a change users rejected, and a rollback

In November 2018 the check-in and calendar UI changed. `NEG_REDESIGN` — 23
reviews (1.29%), mean 3.22★ — is concentrated here.

What users lost, in their words:

> "Instead of a single tap to log a habit for the day, you're forced to go through **multiple
> steps** now!" — #1288, `3390321767`, us, 1★
> "changed the check-in marks on the calendar from a **fully filled-in square to a TINY dot**"
> — #1289, `3392908796`, us, 2★
> "Die frühere Logik war besser: grün für heute erledigt und die Uhr zeigt den Wochenerfolg"
> (*the earlier logic was better: green for done today and the ring shows the week's success*)
> — #1296, `3418021543`, de, 3★
> "there's **no visual indicator** telling you that you have completed that habit for the day.
> Therefore, I have ended up clicking the same habit multiple times" — #1291, `3403476071`, us, 3★

**And then the developer rolled it back — and reviewers upgraded their ratings in public.**
This is the corpus's clearest demonstration of responsiveness converting anger into loyalty:

> "It takes now **3 clicks instead of one** to check in for an old day… *UPDATE: Dev Team reacted
> quite quick - the last update has significantly improved the check in experience once again.
> Happy to have single tap check in back… **Changed my rating to 5 stars ;-)**"
> — #1310, `3493496861`, de, edited from 1★ to **5★**
> "*Update x2 (2/4/18): **Thank you for listening!** The little green dot on the calendar has been
> restored to a solid green dot which looks much, much better to me… Thanks!*"
> — #1308, `3491619389`, us, 5★

33 reviews in total confirm a fix or a delivered request
(21 of them before 2019).

## 7.5 The widget: shipped, lost, restored — and never modernised

This is a three-act story and every act is datable.

**Act 1 — shipped, October 2016.** `REQ_WIDGET` runs from 2016-03-10
(#5, `1345876517`, day two of the app's life: *"Why aren't there notification widgets for it? I
use my today screen every day"*). The Notification-Centre widget arrived in October 2016 and was
immediately loved:

> "ウィジット対応 / これはうれしい" (*widget support / this makes me happy*) — #508, `1470744014`, jp, 5★
> "des choses dont je n'avais ni le courage ni la volonté de faire, sont finalement faites grâce
> à Today et **son Widget super bien pensé**" — #538, `1476474960`, fr, 5★

`PR_WIDGET` 28 reviews (1.57%), mean **4.50★**. For many
reviewers it became the *primary* interface — "the main way i used the app" (#1659).

**Act 2 — removed, September 2021.** Version 1.9.55 shipped 2021-09-02. Its release notes
(§E4) mention refactoring and "More updates and new features are coming soon" — and say nothing
about the widget. 11 reviews between 2021-09-05 and 2021-12-31 report it
gone, in six languages:

> "Where is the widget? I lost it after the last update" — #1658, `7774471535`, id, 4★
> "No more widget. **Which is the main way i used the app.** So, I probably won't keep up to be
> using it" — #1659, `7786249554`, us, 1★
> "Позовался виджетом. Было удобно. Теперь он где-то пропал. **Верните**" (*I used the widget. It
> was convenient. Now it's gone somewhere. Give it back*) — #1672, `7895168833`, ua, 5★
> "还我小组件" (*give me back my widget*) — #1676, `7939841492`, cn, 3★

**Act 3 — restored, November 2021.** Version 1.9.57 (2021-11-04): *"The Notification Center
widget has been restored and is now available again for all users!"* (§E4). Ten days later:

> "Thank you for making the widget come back !" — #1678, `8022641382`, fr, 5★

**But the iOS-14 home-screen widget — announced by Apple in June 2020 — was never built.**
Requests for it begin 2020-09-21 and continue to 2025-12-15:

> "now we get widgets, not just locked to one page, and **I NEED a Today widget** on my
> health/goals page" — #1599, `6552481616`, us, 4★ (Oct 2020)
> "any plans on giving us an update for iOS 14. Widgets, Shortcuts integration… **The Twitter
> account isn't really active. The silence is a little concerning.**" — #1594, `6453348017`, us, 4★
> "The app needs a widget for **IOS 26**" — #1776, `13304685452`, es, 5★ (Oct 2025)

Five and a half years of the same request, ending in the most recent era of the corpus.
`REQ_WIDGET` rises from 1.98% of E1 to **16.95% of E5**
— the only request that grows as the corpus shrinks.

## 7.6 What improved, and what it cost to improve it

Not everything decayed. Things that shipped and were confirmed by reviewers:

| Delivered | When | Confirmed by |
|---|---|---|
| Free tier 1 → 3 habits | Mar 2016 | #169, #175, #1000 |
| Multiple check-ins per day | Sep 2016 | #350 — *"Multiple checkins per item per day is what I've been asking for since this app was first released"* |
| Notification-Centre widget | Oct 2016 | #508, #529, #538 |
| 90-day start-date limit removed | Sep 2016 | #409 |
| zh/ja localisation | Sep 2016 | #354, #374 (poorly) |
| Apple Watch app | ~May 2017 | #871, #985 |
| "Manage Data" export/delete | May 2018 | #1177 — *"Kudos for the new 'Manage Data' section… If only more companies would follow suit"* |
| Nov-2018 redesign rolled back | Dec 2018–Feb 2019 | #1308, #1310 |
| Performance fix | Mar & May 2020 | #1525, #1558 — *"The latest update fixed the lag. I've dealt with that tedious issue every single day (multiple times a day) for months"* |
| Dark mode | Oct 2021 (§E4) | requested #1465, #1477 (2019) — **two years later** |
| Widget restored | Nov 2021 (§E4) | #1678 |
| Time-zone check-in bug | Oct 2025 (§E4) | first reported #1376 (Feb 2019) — **six and a half years later** |

**The pattern is latency, not absence.** Almost everything users asked for was eventually built
or fixed. The median gap between a well-supported request and its delivery, where delivery
happened at all, is measured in years — and in several cases (iPad, home-screen widget,
bad-habit tracking, archive, overview screen) delivery never happened at all across ten years
and 1,785 reviews.

## 7.7 What persisted across all six eras

Themes present in **every** era, i.e. never fixed and never stopped mattering:

| Theme | E1 | E2 | E3 | E4 | E5 | E6 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| `PR_DESIGN` | 32.51% | 23.26% | 18.83% | 14.63% | 15.25% | 15.38% |
| `PR_SIMPLE` | 25.09% | 22.41% | 10.06% | 11.38% | 8.47% | 15.38% |
| `U:paywall_complaint` | 16.19% | 5.07% | 6.49% | 12.2% | 6.78% | 15.38% |
| `REQ_DASHBOARD` | 1.73% | 4.44% | 7.47% | 7.32% | 1.69% | 15.38% |

`BUG_SWIPE` (2016-03-11 → 2024-08-11), `BUG_NOTIF`
(2016-04-04 → 2025-02-10) and `REQ_DASHBOARD`
(2016-03-21 → 2026-07-13) each span essentially the entire corpus.

## 7.8 Sensitivity — does any single episode drive the conclusions?

| Scenario | Mean | Δ vs 3.97 |
|---|---:|---:|
| Full corpus | 3.97 | — |
| Excluding the first 90 days (n=302, mean 3.57) | 4.05 | +0.08 |
| Excluding Nov–Dec 2022 (n=12, mean 2.42) | 3.98 | +0.01 |
| Pre-2019 only | 4.13 | — |
| 2019 onward only (n=458) | 3.52 | — |

**No single episode drives the decline.** Removing the botched launch *raises* the mean by only
0.08; removing the 2022 black-screen wave raises it by 0.01. The gap
that matters is pre-2019 (4.13★) versus 2019-onward (3.52★) — a
drop of **0.61 stars** sustained over 458 reviews and seven years.
That is not a bad week; it is the product's trajectory.

95% Wilson intervals for the headline global rates (n=1,785):

| Theme | Rate | 95% CI |
|---|---:|---|
| `PR_DESIGN` | 25.77% | 23.79% – 27.85% |
| `PR_SIMPLE` | 20.22% | 18.43% – 22.15% |
| `U:bug_any` | 26.27% | 24.29% – 28.37% |
| `U:reliability` | 13.11% | 11.62% – 14.75% |
| `BUG_WATCH` | 4.93% | 4.02% – 6.03% |
| `DEV_STALE` | 3.75% | 2.97% – 4.74% |
| `U:free_tier_limit` | 8.12% | 6.94% – 9.48% |
| `MON_PAID_EXPLICIT` | 10.53% | 9.19% – 12.04% |
| `REQ_WIDGET` | 2.63% | 1.99% – 3.48% |
| `PR_OUTCOME` | 5.94% | 4.93% – 7.13% |
| `REQ_DASHBOARD` | 3.92% | 3.12% – 4.93% |
| `U:dataloss_any` | 3.08% | 2.37% – 3.99% |

All are narrow. The findings are not fragile at the global level; they are fragile only inside
E5 and E6, which is stated wherever they are used.

---

# PART 8 — PRODUCT IMPLICATIONS

Every recommendation ties to numbered findings. Ordered by evidence weight × cost of inaction.
"Immediate fix" means a defect users already paid for; "experiment" means a change whose
outcome the corpus cannot predict; "research" means the corpus does not contain the answer.

## 8.1 Immediate fix — make check-in latency independent of history size (§0.4, §3.3, §7.2)

`BUG_PERF` 70 reviews (3.92%, Very strong signal), rising to
16.95% of E5. 17 of those reviewers
(24.3% of the theme) also record leaving or being blocked.

The defect is specific and reviewers have localised it for you: **write latency grows with
accumulated check-ins.** #1756 explicitly diagnoses inefficient storage at ~200 check-ins;
#1716 reports total failure past 1,000; #1382 reports 5 seconds per tap after three years of
use; #1694 reports over a minute.

This is the highest-leverage fix in the report because of *who* it hits: the users with the
longest histories are the ones who proved the product works, are most likely to have paid, and
are most likely to be publicly enthusiastic. The app currently degrades in proportion to its
own success.

**Test it the way users experience it:** seed 20 habits × 1,000 check-ins and measure tap-to-
confirmation. Do not test on a fresh install.

## 8.2 Immediate fix — restore purchases reliably, and make restore discoverable (§5.4)

`MON_RESTORE` 18 reviews (1.01%), 2016-03-28 →
2021-02-12 — **nine years**. Plus `MON_IAP_FAIL` 11 and
`MON_OVERCHARGE` 2.

Reviewers reinstall, lose premium, and conclude they were scammed ("¿Es una estafa?" — #1618).
One found the answer himself in Settings → Restore Purchases (#1263) — which means the
function exists and is undiscoverable. Two independent actions:

1. Fix the underlying receipt-validation failures.
2. **Surface restore at the paywall**, not buried in Settings. Every user hitting the paywall
   who has already paid is a support ticket and a one-star review.

## 8.3 Immediate fix — reminders must fire, and must not stop when a user misses a day (§3.3)

`BUG_NOTIF` 63 (3.53%, Very strong signal), spanning
2016-04-04 → 2025-02-10 — the full life of the app, never resolved.
10 reviewers say it makes the app useless.

One behaviour is reported independently in 2017 and 2021 and deserves a direct test:

> "the app seems to stop its reminders even if you miss just one activity so the following
> day, you don't get a reminder (**surely this is the most important time to have a reminder?**)"
> — #933, `1712510655`, gb, 4★
> "if I don't check in every single day, the reminders stop all together" — #1674, `7936221496`, us, 3★

A habit app that goes quiet precisely when the user lapses has inverted its own purpose.

## 8.4 Immediate fix — never lose data, and never lose it from a paid backup (§3.3, §5.2)

`U:dataloss_any` 55 (3.08%);
12 of those reviewers had paid — **and backup is the
paid feature**. Reports include a wiped three-year history (#1553), a backup overwritten by
the restore flow (#1079), and "lost ALL my information on 2 occasions" (#1592).

Minimum bar: restore must never destroy the backup it is reading; a failed restore must leave
existing data intact; and covers/photos must survive a restore (#1005, #1303).

Given `REQ_ACCOUNT` (3) and the strong `PR_PRIVACY` signal (4),
the right shape is probably **optional** account-based sync — not mandatory, because on-device
data is something reviewers actively praise (§2.4).

## 8.5 Immediate fix — ship the iOS home-screen widget (§7.5)

`REQ_WIDGET` 47 (2.63%, Meaningful signal), and it is the **only request
that grows as the corpus shrinks** (1.98% of E1 →
16.95% of E5). Home-screen widget requests begin 2020-09-21
and run to 2025-12-15.

The evidence that this converts is unusually direct:

> "Les urgen widget y **les juro pago el premium**" (*they urgently need a widget and I swear
> I'd pay premium*) — #1749, `10949219510`, mx, 4★
> "its lack of a widget means **I'll probably need to look for something else**" — #1763, `12176761820`, gb, 4★

And the NC widget's history proves the value: when it existed, `PR_WIDGET` (28,
mean 4.50★) described it as the primary interface; when it vanished for two months
in 2021 it produced 11 complaints in six languages.

## 8.6 Experiment — let a habit be paused, archived, or legitimately skipped (§3.4)

`REQ_ARCHIVE` 11 + `REQ_SKIP` 11 = 21 reviews, and
`REQ_ARCHIVE` carries the corpus's most-upvoted request (#1087, 22 up / 36).

The current design forces a user who has *succeeded* to delete their habit and destroy the
record of succeeding. That is a strange thing for a habit app to do.

Two distinct needs, both real:

1. **Archive** — "已经形成或者没必要了，但是目前只能删除，结果所有数据会跟着没有" (#1087).
2. **Skip** — a day legitimately not counted (illness, travel, a bad mental-health day). #755
   asks for it while sick; #1163 during a holiday; **#762 uses it to manage depressive
   episodes** and describes it as the reason the app worked for them.

A *skip* feature already exists and **nobody understands it** — #1532, #1584 and #1299 each ask
what it does, and #1584 reports it damaging streak statistics. Fixing the explanation may be
worth more than building anything.

## 8.7 Experiment — resolve the "bad habits" promise (§3.4)

The store listing says "**Quit bad habits** and stay on track" (§E1). The product cannot track
a limit. `REQ_BREAKBAD` is 15 reviews (0.84%), `USE_QUIT` shows
16 reviewers trying anyway, and #1559 asks outright what the description even means.

Two honest options, and the corpus does not say which is better:

- **Build it** — a "maximum N per period" habit type, as #1216, #1223, #1244, #1245 and #1651
  all describe, and as competitors *Done* and *HabitBull* are named as already having.
- **Stop claiming it** — remove the promise from the listing.

What is not defensible is the current state: advertising a capability, collecting users who
need it, and having them discover the gap after purchase.

## 8.8 Experiment — give the one-page-per-habit model an escape hatch (§3.3)

`REQ_DASHBOARD` 70 (3.92%), 2016-03-21 →
2026-07-13, plus `BUG_SWIPE` 39 and `UX_FRICTION` 41.

The full-screen-photo model is the differentiator (§0.1) **and** the scaling problem. Do not
remove it; make it optional past a threshold. Users have specified the fix repeatedly:

> "**list as a default view**; tap on an item will add a point; a habit image as a part of a row"
> — #1494, `5294830409`, ua, 3★
> "起動した時に、タスクの一覧画面を表示できるようにしてほしい" (*I want the task list shown on launch*) — #1300, `3440039586`, jp, 4★
> "the ability to not use a picture for every habit but rather just a simply background **to make
> the app faster**" — #1040, `2026175358`, au, 1★

Note that #1040 connects the visual model directly to §8.1's performance problem.

## 8.9 Experiment — re-examine the free tier, with the corpus's own evidence (§0.6)

The free tier has been 1 → 3 → 5 → 1 habits. The evidence is one-directional:

- At **1 habit**: `MON_FREE1` 78 reviews at mean **1.97★**, of which
  71 arrived in 2016 alone, and the complaint returned within months of the
  cap being restored in ~2022.
- At **3 habits**: `MON_FREE3` 67 reviews at mean **3.60★** — the same
  grievance, 1.63 stars higher.
- At **3–5 habits**: **0 one-habit complaints in four years.**

`MON_TRIAL_REQ` (33) is the argument reviewers actually make, and they make it
identically in 2016 and 2024: one habit is not a trial, it is a screenshot. A one-habit cap in
an app whose paid value is *multi-habit dashboards* prevents the prospect from ever seeing the
thing they would be buying.

⚠️ **Caveat, stated because it matters:** the 1★ reviews at a 1-habit cap come disproportionately
from people who never used the app. Removing them may raise the rating without raising revenue.
The corpus cannot settle that; §8.13 says how to.

## 8.10 Immediate fix — the $299.99 tier needs an explanation or a change (§0.7, §E2)

External: the listing shows $3.99/month, $9.99/year, and **$299.99 for "All premium features"**
(§E2). Reviewers cannot parse it and say so:

> "why is Today Premium $10 per year, but ALL Premium Features are $300 ❓ **what is the
> difference** between Today Premium and ALL Premium Features❓" — #1704, `8946229677`, us, 2★

$299.99 is 30× the annual price. Whatever the intent — a lifetime tier, a price-anchoring
device, a legacy artefact — **it currently reads to users as either a mistake or contempt**, and
it sits next to a history of paying customers losing their purchase (§8.2). Either explain it
at the point of sale or remove it.

`MON_SUBSCRIPTION` is 8 reviews, all post-2022, **none positive**. Against
that, `PR_ONETIME` is 23 reviews at mean 4.61★ praising the model that
was replaced. That asymmetry is worth weighing before defending the change.

## 8.11 Immediate fix — answer support, and be seen to (§3.6)

`SUP_NORESP` 25 (1.40%) against `PR_DEV` 39 (2.18%,
mean 4.59★) — and 30 of the 39 praise reviews are from before
2019. `FIX_CONFIRM` is 33 reviews, 21 of them pre-2019.

This corpus contains repeated, documented instances of a reply converting a 1★ into a 5★
(#1310, #1308, #1038, #1033, #1081). It also contains a user discovering the support email
address in the app is **misspelled** (#1770, `12543093674`) and getting no reply to the
corrected one. That is the cheapest fix in this report.

## 8.12 What the corpus says to copy — and not to copy

**Copy:**
- **The full-screen personal photo per habit.** The single most-credited mechanism in
  1,785 reviews (97 direct, 523 in union). Reviewers
  explain it as motivational, not decorative.
- **A per-habit configurable dashboard.** `PR_CARDS` mean 4.69★. It is what makes
  power users pay and what makes them stay across a decade of defects.
- **One-time pricing, honestly priced.** `PR_ONETIME` mean 4.61★, and it is named as
  the reason for choosing this app over rivals.
- **Visible responsiveness.** §7.4 is a case study in a rollback earning public rating upgrades.
- **No ads, no account, on-device data.** Quiet assets (7, 4,
  1) that cost nothing and are named unprompted.

**Do not copy:**
- **One screen per habit as the only navigation.** It does not survive 10+ habits (§8.8).
- **Selling backup and then losing data** (§8.4).
- **Gating the biometric lock** — and then shipping a build where enabling it crashes the app
  (§7.3 wave 3).
- **A visual design whose data layer scales worse than the visuals** (§8.1).
- **Advertising a capability you do not have** (§8.7).
- **Letting an unlaunchable build sit for 1,030 days** (§0.8). This is what converts a
  category-leading app into "abandonware" — the word reviewers themselves reach for (#1770).

## 8.13 Research questions the corpus cannot answer

1. **Does a wider free tier raise revenue, or only the rating?** §8.9 shows the complaint
   vanishes; it cannot show what happened to conversion. Run it as an A/B test with revenue,
   not rating, as the metric.
2. **Who actually pays?** 18 reviewers name a trigger; that is a sample of
   reviewers, not of buyers. Instrument the paywall.
3. **What is the real retention curve?** 98 reviews announce leaving. Silent
   churn is invisible here and is certainly larger.
4. **Do cards drive retention or just enthusiasm?** They correlate with the highest-rated
   reviews (mean 4.72★) and with confusion (§0.2). Measure card usage
   against 90-day retention.
5. **Is there an accessibility problem?** 2 mentions in 1,785
   reviews. That is an evidence gap, not an all-clear — a full-screen-photo app with text over
   imagery has obvious contrast risk, and #381 already reported unreadable text on light
   wallpapers in 2016.
6. **Can the 2025–26 revival recover the user base?** E6 is 13 reviews. Re-run this
   analysis in twelve months.

---

# PART 9 — EVIDENCE APPENDIX

## 9.A Counting rules

1. **Denominator.** Global = 1,785. Country = that country's reviews. Star band = that
   band's reviews. Era = that era's reviews. Paid segment = 188. Every percentage in
   this report states which.
2. **Non-exclusive.** A review carrying eight ideas is counted in eight themes. Percentages sum
   past 100%. They are *shares of reviews mentioning a thing*, never shares of a pie.
3. **One code per review per theme.** A review that mentions crashes four times is one
   `BUG_CRASH`.
4. **Hand-coded from the text.** No code was ever inferred from the star rating. A 5★ review
   whose text is a bug report is coded as a bug (and flagged `CONTRA_RATING`).
5. **Praise requires a reason.** `PR_GENERIC` is for "great app" with no stated reason and is
   never used as evidence of a specific strength.
6. **Requests vs defects.** "It doesn't have X" is `REQ_*`. "X doesn't work" is `BUG_*`.
   "I don't understand X" is `UX_CONFUSE`. "X isn't useful to me" is `NEG_FEATURE_UNUSED`.
   These are kept apart deliberately (prompt §2.3).
7. **`MON_PAID_EXPLICIT` requires a statement of payment**, not an inference from feature use.
   "I use the premium cards" does not qualify; "I bought premium" does.
8. **Unions are derived, never hand-applied**, and are exactly the roll-up of their members
   (verified: §9.C).
9. **Mechanical codes** (`STAR_n`, `EDITED`, `VOTED`, era) come from the record, never judgement.
10. **Signal bands** are computed from count ÷ denominator using the thresholds in §1.5, not
    assigned.

## 9.C Validation — 636 checks, and what they caught

`86-validate.py` runs **636 checks; all pass.** Coverage:

| Group | Checks | What it proves |
|---|---:|---|
| Coverage and identity | 9 | all 1,785 classified; index is a bijection onto 1…1,785; country/rating/date/title re-read from `reviews.jsonl` and compared |
| Codes | 5 | no unknown code survives the build; no alias survives; no intra-review duplicates; every review has ≥1 hand code; **every one of the 166 defined codes is used** |
| `THEME_TO_IDS` consistency | 386 | for each of 199 codes: no duplicate IDs, and the ID list agrees with `CLASSIFICATION` in both directions |
| Mechanical codes | 5 | `STAR_n` matches `rating`; star totals reconcile against `manifest.json`; `EDITED`/`VOTED` match the record |
| Unions | 25 | each union recomputed from its members and compared set-for-set |
| Semantic/timeline sanity | 13 | see below |
| Bounds | 193 | no code claims more reviews than exist |
| Language plausibility | 2 | every `zh`/`ja` tag contains the relevant script or sits in the matching storefront |

**The timeline checks are the important ones.** They were written before the era analysis and
would have failed if any finding in Part 7 were back-fitted:

- `PR_WATCH` and `BUG_WATCH` cannot predate the Watch app (Apr 2017) — **pass**.
- `PR_WIDGET` cannot predate the widget (Sep 2016) — **pass**.
- `MON_SUBSCRIPTION` cannot predate 2022 — **pass**.
- `REQ_MULTIDAILY` must concentrate before its Sep-2016 release — **pass**.
- `DEV_STALE` must be >80% post-2018 — **pass**.
- `MON_FREE1` must split into a 2016 wave and a 2022+ tail with a gap — **pass**.

### What earlier runs caught

**During reading**, the per-batch checker (`86-check-cls.py`, run after every 100 reviews)
caught three ID-alignment drifts — cases where I mis-aligned the ID column against the review
text, which would have silently attributed every quote in that block to the wrong review:

| Batch | Symptom | Reviews affected |
|---|---|---:|
| `0001-0100` | IDs shifted by one from #81 | 20 |
| `1201-1300` | #1232 written before #1231 | 2 |
| `1301-1400` | IDs shifted by one from #1338 | 3 |

All corrected against the batch dump before the next batch was read.

**The first full validation run failed two checks — both were defects in my checks, not in the
data**, and both are recorded here rather than quietly fixed:

1. `MON_FREE1 and MON_FREE3 never co-occur` failed on **#169** (`1351843491`), who narrates the
   free-tier change itself and legitimately carries both. The check was replaced with one that
   asserts the co-occurrence happens **exactly once, and only at #169**.
2. `every MON_PRICE_NAMED review mentions a numeral` failed on **#167** and **#1019**, both
   Chinese, both writing the price in CJK numerals (`三十大洋`, `三十块钱` — thirty yuan). The check
   assumed ASCII digits and was widened.

Neither failure changed a code.

### What validation cannot check

Whether a code was applied *correctly* to a given review. That is hand judgement. Every line in
`86-cls/*.txt` carries a note quoting the text that drove it, so it is auditable — but not
machine-verifiable.

## 9.D Sensitivity

| Scenario | Mean | Δ |
|---|---:|---:|
| Full corpus (1,785) | 3.97 | — |
| Excluding first 90 days (n=302) | 4.05 | +0.08 |
| Excluding Nov–Dec 2022 (n=12) | 3.98 | +0.01 |
| Pre-2019 | 4.13 | — |
| 2019 onward (n=458) | 3.52 | −0.61 vs pre-2019 |

No single episode drives the conclusions. The pre-2019 / post-2019 split does, and it is
sustained over 458 reviews and seven years.

95% Wilson intervals are given in §7.8 for the twelve headline rates. All are narrow at
n=1,785. The fragile numbers in this report are exclusively those computed inside
E5 (59) and E6 (13), and every one is flagged where it appears.

## 9.E External sources — all accessed 13 September 2026

| # | Source | Used for |
|---|---|---|
| E1 | `https://itunes.apple.com/lookup?id=1055295863&country=<cc>` — 11 storefronts | identity, version, release date, minimum OS, size, localisations, category |
| E2 | US App Store product page (`apps.apple.com/us/app/id1055295863`) | the three in-app purchase price points |
| E3 | Same, per storefront | public rating mean and count per storefront |
| E4 | Apple version history (embedded in the product page) — 25 versions, 2020-03-12 → 2026-02-13 | release dates, release notes, the two long gaps |
| E5 | Apple privacy label, US listing | data-collection declarations |

Raw captures: `86-itunes-*.json`, `86-us-listing.html`, `86-version-history.tsv`, `86-external.md`.

**Externally sourced facts used in this report** (kept separate from review evidence throughout):

| Fact | Value |
|---|---|
| First release | 2016-03-08 |
| Current version / date | 1.18 / 2026-02-13 |
| Minimum OS | iOS 18.0 |
| Price: lifetime / annual / monthly | $299.99 / $9.99 / $3.99 |
| Release gap, 1.9.54 → 1.9.55 | 374 days |
| Release gap, 1.9.63 → 1.10 | **1,030 days** |
| Releases in 2023 / 2024 / 2025 | 0 / 0 / 9 |
| Public rating range across 11 storefronts | 4.52 – 4.78 (vs corpus 3.97) |

**The version history is load-bearing for §0.8, §7.3 and §7.5** — it is what converts "users say
the app was abandoned" into "the app shipped a build that would not launch and then shipped
nothing for 1,030 days", and what confirms the widget removal (1.9.55) and restoration
(1.9.57, whose notes say so explicitly).

## 9.F Absence tests — patterns swept for across all 1,785 reviews

Each is a **deliberately broad recall sweep** (word-boundary regex across title + body, in all
relevant languages), then every hit was read. A broad sweep over-matches by design; the "Reading"
column reports what the hits actually say.

| Pattern swept for | Reviews matching | % | Reading |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| ads in app | 14 | 0.78% | No one complains about advertising in Today, because there is none. The substantive mentions **praise its absence** (`PR_NOADS` 7). |
| AI / machine learning | 12 | 0.67% | Essentially absent. One 2016 reviewer wanted predictive nagging (#376). No user asks for AI coaching in ten years. |
| social / friends / share | 16 | 0.90% | Absent, and **contested**: #391 calls the lack of community a fatal flaw, #911 argues explicitly against adding it. |
| subscription | 21 | 1.18% | Only appears from 2022 onward, and always negatively (`MON_SUBSCRIPTION` 8). |
| refund | 3 | 0.17% | Three reviews. Two report Apple refusing the refund (#1103, #1169). |
| privacy / data | 5 | 0.28% | Five reviews, four positive. Privacy is a quiet asset, not a concern. |
| accessibility | 2 | 0.11% | Two incidental mentions. **No accessibility feedback exists in this corpus** — a gap in the evidence, not evidence of a gap. |
| children / family | 1 | 0.06% | One review (#996) on Family Sharing not covering IAPs. |
| streak anxiety / guilt | 7 | 0.39% | Seven. Notably #969 praises that it *doesn't* nag, and #762 uses skip-days to manage depressive episodes. |
| export / data ownership | 54 | 3.03% | 54 mentions. A real, persistent, unmet demand (`REQ_EXPORT` 14 plus incidental mentions). |
| dark mode | 2 | 0.11% | Two requests (Oct/Nov 2019). Shipped Oct 2021 — two years later. |
| android | 1 | 0.06% | One mention (#1485), from a user who left for Android and came back. |

Two absences are worth stating as findings:

- **No one asks for AI.** Across ten years and 1,785 reviews, in a category now saturated
  with AI coaching, there is no demand for it here. The one adjacent request (#376, 2016) asks
  for predictive reminder timing.
- **Social features are contested, not merely absent.** #391 calls the lack of community
  "硬伤" (a fatal flaw); #911 argues the opposite — "不需要有什么社交功能…如果真的很懒怎么互相鼓励都
  不管用" (*it doesn't need social features… if you're really lazy no amount of mutual
  encouragement helps*). 16 mentions total. This is not a gap the corpus asks
  to be filled.

## 9.G Price points named by reviewers

`MON_PRICE_NAMED` = 112 reviews (6.27%). Currencies and values
that appear, with the first review naming each:

| Currency | Values named | First |
|---|---|---|
| USD | $4.99, $5, $6, $7, $10, $300 | #3 (2016-03-10) |
| GBP | £3.99, £4, £5 | #11 (2016-03-10) |
| EUR | €2.99, €4.99, €5, €28.47 (overcharge) | #84 (2016-03-12) |
| CNY | ¥30 (三十块/三十大洋) | #115 (2016-03-15) |
| JPY | ¥200–400, ¥600 | #515 (2016-10-23) |
| CAD | $7 | #29 (2016-03-11) |
| AUD | $8 | #59 (2016-03-11) |
| SEK / MXN / ZAR / INR / GTQ | 50kr, 99–100 pesos, R79, ₹399, Q2340 | #127, #834, #1437, #1484, #1693 |

The US $4.99 unlock is by far the most-named. The two outliers — €28.47 charged against a €5
display (#1229) and $299.99 (#1693, #1704, #1781) — are the only ones that provoked accusations
of bad faith.

## 9.H Burst, solicitation and authenticity checks

| Check | Result | Reading |
|---|---|---|
| Repeat `(country, author)` pairs | **0** | no visible astroturfing within a storefront |
| Identical `(country, author, title, body)` tuples | **0** | no duplicate submissions |
| Busiest single day | 2016-03-11 — 54 reviews | day 3 of launch; organic Editors'-Choice traffic, not a campaign |
| Busiest month | 2016-03 — 209 reviews (11.71% of corpus) | launch month; mean 3.11★ — a *negative* burst, which is not what solicitation produces |
| First 30 days | 228 reviews (12.77%), mean 3.25★ | as above |
| Reviews with any votes | 254 (14.23%) | low engagement; max 82 votes |
| Edited reviews | 60 (3.36%) | several narrate their own rating change, which is usable evidence (§7.4) |
| `CONTRA_RATING` | 46 (2.58%) | 40 are 5★ reviews whose text is only a complaint |
| `RATING_LEVERAGE` | 16 (0.90%) | reviewers using the star rating as explicit leverage |
| `MISCAT` | 1 | one review (#196) describes a different app entirely |
| In-app prompt naming the app "Toady" | 2 (#141, #193) | a 2016 string bug, quoted because it dates the rating prompt |

**No solicitation pattern is detectable.** There is no cluster of short, same-day, uniformly
5★ reviews; the largest bursts are negative and tied to datable failures. The one authenticity
claim in the corpus is a reviewer pre-emptively denying being paid — "我不是水军" (*I'm not an
astroturfer*, #391) and "And no I wasn't paid to place this review" (#939) — which is weak
evidence in either direction and is recorded, not relied upon.

## 9.I Developer responsiveness — the evidence both ways

| Signal | n | % | Mean ★ | Period concentration |

## 9.M Post-write verification

After the report is written, `86-verify-report.py` re-reads the **written file** and checks it
against `reviews.jsonl` and the classification. This is the step that catches a claim that was
true when computed but got mistyped, mis-attributed or mis-quoted on the way into prose.

| Distinct review IDs cited anywhere in the report, each confirmed to exist in `reviews.jsonl` | 1,785 |
| Distinct `#index` references, each confirmed in range 1…1,785 | 1,785 |
| `(#index, review-id)` pairs on the same line, each confirmed to agree with the corpus | 171 |
| `(#index, id, storefront, star)` attributions, each re-checked against the record | 132 |
| **Verbatim quotation fragments, each located in the review it is attributed to** | **40** |
| §10 rows, each with id, storefront, star, date, language and code list re-checked | 1,785 |
| §9.J theme rows, each id list re-derived from the classification | 164 |
| Inline `` `CODE` n (%) `` claims in prose, each recomputed | 34 |
| Unfilled placeholders | 0 |

### What this step caught

Five defects that survived every earlier check, because every earlier check looked at the
*data* rather than at the *prose*:

1. **#169 (`1351843491`) — an unmarked elision.** I had written *"I gave it 1 star because they
   only gave you 1 habit"*; the reviewer wrote *"I gave it 1 star because **I hated the fact
   that** they only gave you 1 habit"*. Corrected to the full wording.
2. **#1770 (`12543093674`) — a dropped parenthetical.** A bullet was quoted without marking that
   *"(though a web search finds it on Vimeo)"* had been removed. Ellipsis added.
3. **#1770 again — a dropped `[sic]`.** The reviewer wrote `toda[sic]@neybox.com`; I had
   silently normalised it to `toda@neybox.com`, which changes the meaning of the observation.
   Restored.
4. **#762 (`1562859639`) — an inserted comma.** I had added a comma after *"fühlte"* that the
   reviewer did not write. Removed.
5. **#1103 (`2273762627`) — altered terminal punctuation.** I had ended a Chinese quotation with
   *「？」* where the original has *「，」* followed by a further clause. Restored to the original.

None changed a count or a conclusion, but each was a small misrepresentation of what a real
person wrote, and each is the kind of error that a data-level validation suite structurally
cannot see. That is why this step exists and why its failures are recorded rather than
silently fixed.

---
